They're named as the `Modal` voice names Plaits' structure, brightness and damping, and `Decay` rings longer as it rises, as Plaits' damping knob does, so a name that says what it does was chosen over "Damping". `Tune` is the pitch shifter's list of whole semitones, ±24, from the track's note and its `Pitch`, as Warps' oscillator follows them (decision 44), so a step or LFO moves it a semitone at a time. `Material` starts at `40`, in tune, where Plaits' stiffness table is flat to `4B`; below it the partials bunch, above they spread, and Plaits' compensation keeps the third partial at three times the note. `Position` is Rings' and Elements' pickup: each mode is heard by a cosine across the modes, its frequency `00` to `FF` taking it from the end to the middle, half a cycle, as past that it comes back the way it went; it starts at `08`, about Plaits' own 0.015, and at `FF` the even partials drop out. `Width` is Elements' stereo trick: a second pickup wanders on a 0.5 Hz triangle from a tenth of the way along to past the middle, Elements' defaults, and what it hears apart from the first is added to one side and taken from the other, up to Elements' widest, 0.7, at `FF`; it starts at `80`, and `00` is mono. The triangle's rate is fixed, as Elements' is, so it's no setting. It starts all wet. **Modes:** 24, Plaits' count, fixed. **Smoothing:** every setting and the note glide over the smoothing time; the modes are tuned every 24 frames, Plaits' block at 48 kHz, but played a frame at a time, so it adds no delay, and each mode's gains move in a straight line across the block, as Elements moves its position, so `Position`, `Width` and the level never step. After resting, it starts at the note and what's set rather than gliding there from where it stopped. **Rate:** Plaits' Q is for 48 kHz, so every mode's Q is scaled by the rate over 48 kHz, which keeps the decay in seconds the same at any rate (the `Modal` voice doesn't do this yet, so it rings shorter at 96 kHz). The bank is tuned from 16 Hz to 12 kHz; its lowest mode is within 3 cents of the note from `C-1` to `C-7` at 44.1 to 192 kHz. **Level:** what goes in is the middle of its two sides at an eighth, as Rings and Elements turn theirs down, and what comes out is turned down by the root of what the modes together would pass: each mode's gain squared, times its frequency, times its Q, but no more Q than rings for 20 ms, a drum's length. So a band-pass that rings for less than a hit is matched as a filter passing noise, and one that rings for longer as a click it gathers up, whose peak doesn't grow with its Q. Then it's set so a drum at `Decay` `80` comes out as loud as it went in: within about 1 dB, by RMS and peak, at middle C, and by RMS within 1.2 dB from `C-2` to `C-6`, its peaks within 3 dB. A longer `Decay` rings on and builds up, by about 5 dB at `C0` and 10 dB at `FF` against noise bursts four a second, and its peaks stay within about 3 dB. **Bounded:** a tone held at its note can ring tens of dB louder, so Rings' limiter follows the loudest of the two sides and their difference, rising quickly and falling over about a second, its constants changed to take as long at any rate, and turns both down to keep that under full scale, with a soft clip after; what comes out never passes full scale, tested with a square at 12 dB over full scale, every extreme and the note jumping between ends every frame. **Memory:** its modes, their two sets of gains and their ramps come to 1448 bytes, so it comes from the effect pool, as Warps' filter bank does, rather than taking each slot from 944 bytes to about 2.4 KB. It stops working once nothing's gone in or come out for 0.1 s. **Cost:** a throwaway release-mode test on an Apple M5 gave about 20 ns a frame at its defaults, 0.1% of a core at 48 kHz and 0.4% at 192 kHz. Left for later: chords, as Rings splits its modes among up to four notes from its chord table; Rings' brake on the highest partials' decay; Elements' bowed modes; and a way to hold the note fixed, since a sample track's notes also change its sample's speed. Added on 2026-11-08.
63. **AIFF and FLAC load wherever a WAV loads** (#226). Ableton's Core Library, which kits are to come from, has AIFF in 1,373 of its 4,680 sample references, and only 51 of its 145 drum racks are all WAV. They're read with `symphonia` 0.6.1 (MPL 2.0, which GPL 3 allows), with only its `aiff`, `flac`, `wav` and `pcm` features, so no other format or codec is built in. **WAV is unchanged:** it's still read by `hound` first, so every WAV that loaded before loads to the same frames and the golden renders don't move; only the decoding moved into its own function, and making the sound stereo and converting its rate is shared. **Which reader:** the extension says, `.aif`, `.aiff` or `.flac` in any case going to `symphonia`, and anything else to `hound` as before, so a file with no extension still loads as a WAV. Ableton names its AIFFs `.aif`. **A WAV `hound` can't read** goes to `symphonia`, and if that fails too, `hound`'s error is the one shown. Eight of Ableton's WAVs need it: they have a 7-byte `inst` chunk before the data, and `hound` 3.5.1 doesn't skip the pad byte after a chunk of odd length. With it, all 3,571 sample files in the Core Library load, 1,351 AIFF and 2,220 WAV, checked by a throwaway scan. **Scaling:** `symphonia` turns 16-bit and 24-bit integers into floats by dividing by 32,768 and 8,388,608, as the WAV reader does, so an AIFF or FLAC loads to exactly the frames of the same sound in a WAV, at its own rate or converted. Checked-in files of 1,200 frames, 16-bit stereo at 48 kHz and 24-bit mono at 44.1 kHz, made from WAVs by `afconvert` and `ffmpeg`, test it. More than two channels is refused, as for a WAV, and a file that won't decode is an error naming it. **The sample browser** lists `.wav`, `.aif`, `.aiff` and `.flac`, from one list of extensions in `vt-model`, and `load_wav` is now `load_sample`. Left for later: other formats such as MP3 or Ogg, which drum kits rarely use. Added on 2026-11-08.
64. **Where kits and samples are found is a setting for the machine, in `settings.ron` beside `audio.ron`** (#226). Kits are to come from the author's Ableton library, which stays where Ableton keeps it, so a folder relative to wherever the program ran no longer works; `samples/kits` from the working folder is gone. **The file** is in the config folder, `$XDG_CONFIG_HOME/vimtracker` or `~/.config/vimtracker`, and holds two lists of folders, `kits` and `samples`, each looked through in order, a folder that isn't there passed over. Paths are kept as written, so `~/` stays readable, and expanded when used. **Defaults:** `~/Music/vimtracker/kits` and `~/Music/vimtracker/samples`, kits and samples in folders of their own under one, as the M8 and the Polyend Tracker keep theirs on the card. **Made on first run:** with no file, the program writes one with the defaults and a note on each line, so there's one to find and edit while there's no page for them; that page is left for the project page (#223). A list left out keeps its default, a name it doesn't know, such as `kit`, is refused rather than ignored, and a file that won't read uses the defaults and says why on the status line at start. It's read once, at start. **Kits:** `:kit 909` opens the first `909.ron` in the kit folders, so an earlier folder overrides a later one, as a search path does; a name found nowhere is reported at the first folder's path. `:kit` alone lists the kits of every folder in order, and with none says which folder to add one to. **Samples:** `Space 5` lists the project's `samples` folder, then the sample folders, then the folder the program ran from, as before. **The author's 909:** `samples/kits/909.ron` in the repo folder is moved to `~/Music/vimtracker/kits` in the next PR, when it's rewritten in the new kit format, rather than the old folder being looked in too. The module is `vt_tui::config`, as `vt_model::settings` is the songs' list of settings. Added on 2026-11-08.
65. **`Comb` is a slot effect: a comb filter tuned to the track's note, after Vital's** (#50). It's in `dsp/filters/comb.rs`, adapted from Vital's `synthesis/filters/comb_filter.cpp` and `comb_filter.h`, with its Catmull-Rom reads from `synthesis/lookups/memory.h`, GPL 3 or later with Matt Tytel's notice kept, and its tuning follows the way Rings' `dsp/string.cc` (MIT) corrects for its damping filter, not its code. **Name:** `Comb`, as the Digitakt II's `COMB` filters and Vital's are called. **Styles:** Vital has six, three feedback styles (`Comb`, `Flange+`, `Flange-`) by two filter styles (`Low High` and `Band Spread`). Here they're a `Mode` of `Comb` or `Flange`, and the sign of `Feedback`: negative is Vital's `Flange-`, reading half a cycle back and turning what it reads over, so the peaks sit on the odd harmonics alone at the same pitch, and it does the same as a `Comb` (Vital's negative comb reads a whole cycle back, which puts its pitch an octave down; here it stays on the note). `Feedback` `80` is none: a `Comb` is then only the sound, and a `Flange` a flanger standing still. Turning the feedback over crosses from one read to the other over its first sixteenth below `80`, and the mode crossfades, so neither clicks. The filter styles are `Tone` and `Spread`: `Tone` is the low-pass in the loop, from the note at `00` to eight octaves up at `FF`, and `Spread` brings the high-pass up under it, from eight octaves under the note at `FF` to the low-pass itself at `00`, Vital's narrowest band; so `Spread` `FF` is Vital's `Low High` at its low-pass end, `Tone` `FF` with `Spread` about `80` its high-pass end, and anything else its `Band Spread`. Vital's blend of the two at a cutoff, a shelf, is left out. The pair is Vital's: one-pole low-pass and high-pass, turned up by `1 + g_high / g_low` so the peak passes at unity, here exact trapezoidal one-poles rather than Vital's approximate coefficient. **Settings**, in columns of two: `Mode` and `Tune`, `Feedback` and `Tone`, `Spread` and `Mix`, then `Gain`. `Tune` is `Reso`'s ±24 semitones from the track's note and `Pitch`. `Feedback` follows Vital's curve, the loop's gain the square root of how far it's set from `80`, up to Vital's 1; it starts at `F0`, about 0.4 s to ring away at middle C, and like any comb's, a lower note rings longer. `Tone` starts at `80`, keeping four octaves of harmonics, and `Spread` at `FF`. It starts all wet. **Tuning:** Vital reads a cycle back, so its filter's delay pulls its pitch off. Here the read is shortened by the band's phase delay, as Rings' string is, but worked out where each harmonic's pole actually is: a little inside the circle, as far as it loses each time round. Its phase there decides the delay and its gain how far inside, so the two are worked out in turn three times; on the circle alone, a loop losing 30% a cycle came out 15 cents off. Each harmonic needs a slightly different delay, so the delay is the average over a spread of harmonics up to the 64th, weighed by the fourth power of how long each rings, which lets the one that rings longest decide: the fundamental under a low-pass, a higher harmonic in a band or over a high-pass. Then the cubic read's own delay at that frequency is taken off, twice over. Measured from what rings after a click, the harmonic that rings longest is within 3 cents of where it should be from `C-1` to `C-7` at 44.1 to 192 kHz, with `Tone` from `00` to `FF`, narrow bands, high-passes and both signs of feedback, except negative feedback darker than `Tone` `30`, which is gone within a few cycles: measured that way it's up to 12 cents off, but fitting its poles offline puts it within 0.1 cent. **Low end:** with feedback positive, nothing at all, which doesn't move, is one of the loop's peaks too, and a first-order high-pass in the loop doesn't stop what's well under the note going round: it rings as a slow wobble about a twentieth of the note, 13 Hz at middle C, as Vital's does. Vital's high-pass four octaves down also moves the harmonics out of tune with each other by about 17 cents. So the high-pass within the loop goes no lower than eight octaves down, about a cent, and what goes into the line is high-passed first by two one-poles, outside the loop where they can't pull the tuning: two octaves under the note as a `Comb`, and four as a `Flange`, whose notches need what comes out of the line to match the sound. What's heard keeps the sound's own bass. **Bounded:** Vital's hard clip on what's fed back, so nothing runs away at full feedback, then Rings' limiter, as `Reso` has, and a soft clip; tested with a square at 12 dB over full scale at every extreme of mode, feedback, `Tone` and `Spread`, and the note jumping between ends every frame. **Level:** what comes out is turned down by the root of the power a hit would make, across eight frequencies, with as many trips round the loop as fit in 20 ms, as `Reso` matches its level. Vital's own turning down of a comb's input as its feedback rises and a flanger's straight sound is kept for their balance. A drum at the default settings comes out within 2 dB of its own level by RMS and peak from `C-2` to `C-6`, either mode. At full `Feedback` it rings on and builds up, by 1–8 dB RMS, and up to 13 dB with `Tone` at `FF` too, its peaks within 5 dB. The level is worked out as if the drum were white noise, so a thin loop, `Spread` near `80` with `Tone` at `FF`, comes out up to 8 dB louder at low notes, where most of a drum is under its high-pass. **Smoothing:** every setting and the note glide over the smoothing time; the loop is worked out every 64 frames and everything it plays with moves in a straight line across them. A test jumping every setting, the mode, the sign of the feedback and the note every 64 frames finds a largest step of 0.0014 against 0.044 unsmoothed. **Memory:** two lines of 1.25 cycles of 16 Hz, the lowest it's tuned to, rounded up to a power of two: 32 KB at 48 kHz and 128 KB at 192 kHz, from the effect pool, as the flanger's are. **Cost:** a throwaway release-mode test on an Apple M5 gave about 13–16 ns a frame with nothing moving and 20 ns with the note gliding or `Tone` sweeping, against 18 ns for `Reso` in the same test: 0.1% of a core at 48 kHz, 0.4% at 192 kHz. Working the loop out takes about 0.5 µs, so it's only done when something's moved by more than can be heard, and only for the read that's heard. Left for later: Vital's shelf between low-pass and high-pass, and keytracking or a fixed note, as for `Reso`. Added on 2026-11-08.
66. **A kit file says where the kit came from and what each sound is, after the format proposed in #226.** A kit has a `name`, a `source` such as "Ableton Core Library" and a `category` such as "Drum Machines", for the kit picker to group by later (#221), and its `sounds`. Each sound has a `label`, the track's name, a `name` for what it is, its `path`, `variants` for other tones of it, `layers` for velocity layers soft to hard, a `choke` group, a `gain` in dB and a `tune` in semitones. **Only `sounds` and each `path` are needed**, so a kit of labels and paths still reads; a kit's name is its file's name if it doesn't say, and a sound's is its file's. The `Space 5` list shows each sound by label and name, where it showed the file name. **Kept, not used yet:** variants and layers wait for the sample bank (#189) and velocity layers, and choke groups, gain and tune for the change after the importer, which sets a track's choke group, level and pitch from its kit sound; nothing a kit says beyond the label and path changes the sound yet. **Ranges:** choke groups are 1 to 16, as Ableton's are, and `None` or left out for none, in RON's own `Some(1)`, as the issue writes it; tune is up to 48 semitones either way, as far as Simpler's transpose goes; gain is any finite number of dB. A field it doesn't know is refused, as `settings.ron` refuses one (decision 64), so a misspelt `chock` isn't quietly lost, and the error names the sound by its label, or its path when it has none. **Writing:** the file's types, `KitFile` and `SoundFile`, also write, leaving out what's empty or zero, for the importer to come. **The author's 909** moved from `samples/kits/909.ron` in the repo folder to `~/Music/vimtracker/kits/909.ron`, the default kit folder, in this format: its paths are now absolute, into `samples/ableton-909` in the repo folder, where the samples stay; its hats share choke group 1; and the other tones in each sound's folder are its variants. Added on 2026-11-08.

67. **`Cmd-h` `Cmd-j` `Cmd-k` `Cmd-l` nudge a value without moving, in place of Option, and the window is titled `vimtracker`** (M16). Option with the movement keys was an awkward reach for the thumb, and Cmd is easy, so `D-l` and `D-h` act as `=` and `-`, and `D-k` and `D-j` as `+` and `_`, wherever the Option ones did: the phrase grid and its selections, the chain and song views, the track page, the mixer and the audio page. The Option versions are gone rather than kept beside them. `App::key_event` still drops a key pressed with Cmd unless it means something in the current mode, after any keys typed before it, so `Cmd-a` in Insert writes nothing and `Cmd-1` is still ignored; a held `Cmd-l` repeats as any other key does, through the same path. kitty takes `cmd+h` (hide), `cmd+k` (clear to the cursor) and `cmd+l` (clear the last command's output) by default, and only `cmd+j` is free, so the program sets the window title with crossterm's `SetTitle`, to `vimtracker` or `vimtracker: <folder name>`, at start and whenever opening, saving as or `:new` changes the project. kitty's `map --when-focus-on title:vimtracker cmd+l` and so on then unmaps those shortcuts for vimtracker's window alone; `docs/terminal-notes.md` has the lines, and the alternative, `map cmd+l no_op`, which frees them in every window. The title isn't put back on quitting, as crossterm has no way to read or restore it; the shell or kitty sets its own again. Ghostty's `keybind = super+l=unbind` frees a key in every window, as it has no condition on the title, and is unchecked. `Option-1`, `Option-2` and `Option-s` are untouched. Added on 2026-11-08.
68. **`vimtracker kits import-ableton [library] [out]` writes a kit for each of Live's drum racks, pointing into Live's library** (#226). **Defaults:** the library is the Core Library of the newest Live in `/Applications`, by version and then Suite, Standard and the rest, so Live 12 Suite here; out is the first kit folder in `settings.ron` (decision 64), under `Ableton`, and each kit goes in a folder named for its rack's, its category: `Ableton/Drum Machines/909 Core Kit.ron`. **Reading:** every `.adg` under `Racks/Drum Racks`, unzipped with `flate2` 1.1.10 and read with `roxmltree` 0.21.2, both MIT or Apache 2.0, small, with no dependencies past `flate2`'s Rust `miniz_oxide`. A pad is a `DrumBranchPreset` of the outermost drum rack, so the 62 racks Live wraps in an instrument rack for their macros read the same; its note is 128 less its `ReceivingNote`, and sounds are in note order, C1 first, as Live's pads go. Its samples come only from its instruments: Simpler's and Sampler's sample parts and Drum Sampler's sample, not the reverb impulses some racks also name. **What it plays:** a pad's samples are sorted by zone, their key range and the chain and sample selectors above them. The zone that covers the note the pad sends, usually C3, at the lowest selectors, is what it plays; the hardest of its velocity layers is the sound's `path`, and when it has more than one, all of them, soft to hard, are its `layers`. Every other zone, another key or selector, gives its hardest layer as a `variant`, as do samples stacked on the main one at the same velocities, which sound together in Live. So the 909's bass drum has its four soft-to-hard layers and three other tunings as variants. **Names:** the pad's name, which Live leaves empty on every pad here, then its first instrument's, such as "Bass Drum", then its sample's file name, as most of the Sampled kits need. **Labels:** guessed from the name's words, then the sample's path's, after the Rytm: `hihat` or `hat` gives `CH`, or `OH` with `open`; then `ride` `RD`, `crash` or `cymbal` `CY`, `cowbell` `CB`, `clap` `CP`, `rim` `RS`, `snare` `SD`, `tom` `LT` `MT` or `HT` by `low`, `mid` or `hi`, and `kick` or `bass drum` `BD`. Hats come first so a hat's `hi` isn't a tom's, and a tom that says no more gets no label. A label goes on the lowest pad that earns it, so a second kick is known by its name alone. **Choke** is the pad's choke group, 1 to 16. **Gain** is the instrument's volume over where Simpler and Drum Sampler start, -12 dB, where 696 of the library's 1,366 Simplers sit, plus the pad's mixer volume in dB, to a tenth; **tune** is its transpose, with its fine tune, and the semitones between the note the pad sends and the sample's root key, to the nearest semitone and within ±48. Chain mixers inside a pad, macros and envelopes aren't read. **Left out:** pads with no sample, synths such as Drift and Meld, and a sample that isn't in the library is reported and its part passed over; a rack with no sample on any pad writes no kit. **Re-running:** each file it writes starts with a comment saying the importer made it. It replaces only files that start so, leaves any other file in its way alone and says so, and removes its own files for racks that have gone, so it can be run again after a Live update and touches nothing else. **The kit picker** now looks in folders inside the kit folders, four deep, as the sample browser does, and lists a kit by its path from its kit folder, such as `Ableton/Drum Machines/909 Core Kit`, which `:kit` takes; the grouping by source and category is #221's. **Run for real** on 2026-11-08 into `~/Music/vimtracker/kits/Ableton`: 138 kits with 2,207 sounds, from 145 racks and their 2,323 pads; 116 pads play a synth, the 7 racks of nothing else make no kit, and every sample named was found. Added on 2026-11-08.
69. **`Vowel` is a slot effect: a voice's five formants, band-passes fixed in pitch, morphing from vowel to vowel, after Plaits' speech synth and Vital's formant filter** (#50). It's in `dsp/filters/vowel.rs`, with the vowel table from Plaits' `speech/naive_speech_synth.cc`, MIT, Émilie Gillet's notice kept; Vital's formant filter (GPL 3) was read for how a bank of band-passes filters a sound rather than a pulse, and none of its code is used. **Name:** `Vowel`, a whole word as the other effects' names are, rather than `Vowl` or `Fmnt`. **Table:** Plaits' five vowels by five registers, each with five formants' pitches and levels, rather than Vital's four vowels on an XY pad with four formants each. It's MIT, has a fifth formant, and its registers give a second axis that means something, so `Vowel` and `Size` are two plain settings that steps lock and LFOs move, rather than an X and a Y whose corners are vowels. Vital's two layouts, `AOIE` and `AIUO`, each leave a vowel out; one line through all five leaves none out. **Settings**, in columns of two: `Vowel` and `Size`, `Res` and `Track`, then `Mix` and `Gain`. `Vowel` has `A` at `00`, `E` at `40`, `I` at `80`, `O` at `C0` and `U` at `FF`, so each sits on a round hex value, and blends each formant's pitch in semitones and its level in a straight line from one to the next, as Plaits does; its value reads as `A`, `A>E` and so on. `Size` is Plaits' register, the smallest voice at `00` and the deepest at `FF`, at the same five places, blended the same way, so the formants lower as the voice grows. The two blend together, as Plaits blends its table in both directions. `Res` is the formants' Q, one for all of them as Plaits has it, from 2 at `00` to 30 at `FF`, evenly in ratio: Plaits' 20 is at about `D9`, and 30 is Vital's most. It starts at `80`, a Q of 7.7. `Track` moves the formants with the track's note and `Pitch` from middle C, none at `00`, the default, as fixed formants are what make a vowel, to a semitone a semitone at `FF`. It starts as an `A` in the middle voice, all wet. **Filters:** the track filter's TPT state-variable filter, its step now shared as `Svf::tick_tuned` so the five can share their tuning between the two sides; each band-pass is scaled by its damping so its centre passes at the table's level, and the five are added, as Plaits and Vital add theirs. **Smoothing:** every setting and the note glide over the smoothing time; the formants are worked out every 32 frames, and their tuning, damping and gains move in a straight line across them, with each one's `1 / (1 + g (g + k))` worked out every frame while they move. A test jumping every setting from end to end and the note every 64 frames finds a largest step of 0.0015 against 0.016 unsmoothed. **Level:** band-passes pass only a share of a sound, so what comes out is turned up by the root of how much of pink noise from 20 Hz to 20 kHz the formants pass: each formant's level squared times a band-pass's share of it at that Q, about `pi / 2Q`. Pink noise comes out within 1.2 dB of its own level at every vowel and voice, and at `Res` `00`, `80` and `FF`. Matching it to pink noise, as music's long-run spectrum roughly is, rather than to the white noise `Reso` and `Comb` match a drum to, means a saw at `80` comes out from 7.7 dB under to 5.9 dB over from `C-2` to `C-5`, louder where a harmonic sits on a formant and quieter where none does, and a drum of white noise 6–12 dB quieter, as most of its power is above the formants. Since the gain depends on the levels and Q alone, and the first formant is always at full level, sweeping `Vowel` doesn't pump. **Bounded:** at `Res` `FF` a tone on a formant comes out about 20 dB up, so Rings' limiter, as `Reso` and `Comb` have, keeps it within full scale, with its soft clip; tested with a square at 12 dB over full scale at every extreme of `Vowel`, `Size`, `Res` and `Track` at 44.1 and 192 kHz, with the note jumping between ends every frame. **Tuning:** each vowel's formants are checked against the table by the frequency response from a click, at every vowel and voice at full `Res`: what comes out is the table's shape, the band-passes as analog ones through the bilinear transform, within 0.02 dB at each formant and between them, and every formant that stands out from the others' skirts peaks within 4 cents of its pitch. **Memory:** its five filters on each side, the formants' ramps and its smoothers come to 368 bytes, kept in every slot as an EQ's bands are, which takes a slot from 944 bytes to 1312. **Cost:** a throwaway release-mode test on an Apple M5 gave about 12 ns a frame standing still and 15 ns with `Vowel` sweeping, against 10 ns for `Comb` and 11 ns for `EQ3` in the same test: under 0.1% of a core at 48 kHz. Left for later: a curve on the FX page, as `EQ3` has; Vital's spread, which pulls the formants towards each other; and a formant shift beyond Plaits' registers. Added on 2026-11-08.
70. **A track has a choke group and a pitch of its own, and a kit sound sets them with its level** (#226). **Choke:** a track's `Choke` is `Off` or a group from 1 to 16, as Ableton's are. When a note starts on a track in a group, from a step, a delayed step or an audition, every other sound track in the group fades what it's sounding over the declick time, as a new note on a track fades its last; so a closed hat cuts an open one, as on Live's drum racks and the Rytm's. The track that plays is never cut by its own group, notes that tie on start nothing so cut nothing, and the track page's keyboard previews don't choke, as they play on voices of their own. Two in a group on the same step leave the later track's. It's read from the published song on the audio thread, with no allocation. **Pitch:** the track's `PTCH` setting, which only modulation and steps moved, now has a base, the track's `Pitch`, a semitone a step either side of `80` with a fine part in cents, as a step's `PTCH` value reads; LFOs, envelopes and steps move it from there. **On the Sound page** they're in the right-hand column, under `Swing`: `Pitch` with a centred bar, and `Choke`, which `=` and `-` step through, `x` turns off, and `i` takes as a number or `off`. **From a kit:** `:kit name all`, and choosing a kit's sound with `Space 5`, set each track's `Level` from the sound's gain, a quarter of a dB a step from unity `E0` up to `F8`, its `Pitch` from its tune, and its `Choke` from its group, with the sample and name, as one step to undo. A sound with none of them sets the level back to `E0`, the pitch to `80` and the choke off, so a track takes the kit's sound as it is, not the last one's settings. Added on 2026-11-08.
71. **`Dirty` is a track filter type: Vital's Dirty filter, a 24 dB low-pass whose resonance loop saturates** (#50), ported from `dirty_filter.cpp`, which is GPL 3 or later, keeping Matt Tytel's notice. It's in `dsp/filters/dirty_filter.rs`, and shares the diode ladder's one-pole, Vital's `tanh` and the complex numbers for its curve, which come from the same source. **Circuit:** two trapezoidal one-poles, then two more into a sum through Vital's `tanh`, which is the output. The sum goes round through a one-pole low-pass, and what that takes out through a second, a band-pass, both with Vital's `quickTanh` on their states, and comes back into the sum, times the resonance `r`. Vital's solve for this sample's feedback is kept as it is, so the loop still hears its states a sample late. The one-poles take the exact trapezoidal coefficient, `g / (1 + g)`, as the MS-20's do, in place of Vital's cheaper warp of it, which runs flat at high cutoffs. **Styles:** only the 24 dB low-pass, as `Dirty`, which keeps the list at nine; Vital's 12 dB style is the same filter without its first two one-poles, and its blend from low-pass through band-pass to high-pass, and its notch styles, are other mixes of the same outputs, so either could become a setting on `Dirty` later at no cost, rather than more types. Vital's 24 dB style feeds its first two one-poles back through states its plain one-poles never write, so that feedback is always zero there; it's left out, with the gain meant to balance it, which only lifted high cutoffs by up to 2.4 dB. **Resonance:** `r` runs from 0.1 to 2.15 on the square root of `Res`, eased off at high cutoffs, as Vital has it; it starts to ring by itself at about 2, which is `Res DB` at low cutoffs, earlier than `Ladder` and the MS-20's `ED`, and later at high cutoffs, where it's eased. **Drive:** as Vital has it, `Drive` turns up only what comes straight into the sum, from 1 to 8 as for the others, divided by `1 + r^2 / 2` so a high resonance isn't swamped, and adds up to 0.05 to `r`. The output is turned down by the square root of the drive, as the others are. As what comes straight in is only a gain on a quiet signal, the extra resonance is the only way drive changes the shape of its response, and unlike #218's choice for the MS-20 it's kept, as it's this filter's character, and the curve takes it: `response_db` now takes the drive, which only `Dirty` reads, and draws the resonance it adds but not the level, as the others leave out drive's level. **Tuning:** under where it rings by itself, the peak is at `Cut`, within 5 cents from `Res D9` and 15 from `CC`, and falls under it with less, as a two-pole's does. Past it, its saturating one-poles slow as it rings louder, so it rang up to 60 cents flat; the loop's coefficient is raised by `0.4 e / (1 - G)^3`, where `e` is how far `r` is past where it starts to ring, worked out from where its poles reach the unit circle, which keeps its ringing within 3 cents of `Cut` from 50 Hz to 16 kHz, with or without drive. Under that it changes nothing, so the curve is untouched where it can be seen. **Level:** a quiet signal with no resonance comes through at unity; Vital's 0.1 of resonance at the bottom and its `tanh`'s slope of 1.004 are taken out. A 0.7 saw, open, loses 1.8 dB to its saturation, where `Ladder` loses 0.3 and `MS20 LP` 0.8, so it's a little crunched even at `Drive 00`. The bass at 100 Hz, with `Cut` at 1 kHz, is 3.1 dB down at `Res 40`, 5.4 at `80` and 6.8 at `C0`, where `Ladder`'s is 6.3, 9.9 and 12.4. Ringing by itself, it peaks at about 0.36, about `MS20 LP`'s 0.37, where `Ladder`'s is 0.13, and the output stays within 0.8. **Curve:** `dirty_filter::response`, worked out exactly from the filter's own updates in the z-plane, with the sample of lag left in, and through the slope of its `tanh`, which matches what comes out to within 0.1 dB. **Aliasing:** a 5 kHz sine driven hard puts its 9th and 7th harmonics, folded to 3 and 13 kHz, at -68 and -71 dB. **Cost:** a throwaway release-mode test on an Apple M5, one channel driven, gave 52 ns a sample for `Dirty`, 49 for `Ladder`, 61 for `Diode`, 31 for `MS20 LP` and 25 for a driven `LP`; its settings are worked out each sample, as drive moves them. Added on 2026-11-08.

72. **`Mode` is one shared filter setting that gives the types their other responses, so the list stays at nine** (#50), from the research comment's small add-ons and decisions 61 and 71. It's `FilterMode` in `song.rs`, `Track.filter_mode`, and the mixes are in `dsp/filters/filter.rs`. **What it means:** `LP`, `HP`, `BP` and `Notch` offer `12 dB` and `24 dB`, as tracker-ce's multimode filter cascades two state-variable filters for its 24 dB low-pass. The second filter has no resonance of its own for a low-pass or high-pass, so the peak stays as high as at 12 dB rather than doubling in dB, and at `Res 00` the pair is a Linkwitz-Riley, 6 dB down at `Cut`; a band-pass or notch has the first's, as their peak and dip don't grow with resonance, so they get narrower and wider. `Ladder` and `Dirty` offer `LP24`, `LP12`, `BP`, `HP12`, `HP24` and `Notch`. The ladder's are Vital's `LadderFilter` weights on what goes into its first stage and each stage's output, such as `{1, -4, 6, -4, 1}` for `HP24` and `{0, 0, -1, 2, -1}` for `BP`, whose sign keeps its peak in step with the others'; `Notch` is Vital's notch-pass-swap at its middle, `{1, -4, 8, -8, 4}`, which is `((1 - L)^2 + L^2)^2`. `Dirty`'s are Vital's 24 dB and 12 dB styles: each of its two two-poles gives low-pass, band-pass or high-pass from its input and one-poles, and its 12 dB modes pass straight through the one before the loop, as Vital's `process12` leaves it out. `BP` is the 24 dB style's, 12 dB an octave either side, rather than offering both. `Diode` offers `Full`, Vital's lowest `2^-9`, as `Acid` and the diode had it, and a low cut 3, 2 and 1 octaves under `Cut`. Vital's second diode style sweeps that high-pass with a knob; four steps were chosen over a continuous setting, so `Mode` stays one list choice, the steps follow `Cut` as Vital's does, and the utility filter's `Low cut` is already a continuous low cut. The MS-20's offer none: their 12 dB and 6 dB responses are the circuit, and `Mode` shows `--`, dimmed, as the track page dims what doesn't apply, with `=` and `K` saying the type has none. **Levels:** a 24 dB band-pass passes a quarter at its peak with no resonance, 12 dB down, so `BP` on the ladder and on `Dirty` is turned up by the low-pass's bass over what it passes at `Cut`, up to 4 and down to 1 as resonance makes the peak the loudest part: it peaks as loud as `LP24` is at its loudest, within 0.6 dB with no resonance and 0.5 at `Res CC`. On the ladder that's `|4 - k| / (1 + k)`; on `Dirty` it's worked out from its response at `Cut` and at DC every 32 frames, and goes on the two-pole before the loop, so the loop saturates as it would for a low-pass. The high-passes pass the top at unity, as the low-passes pass the bass; `Dirty`'s top falls with `Res` as its bass does, as its resonance takes from what comes straight in. `LP12` and `HP12` on the ladder ring twice as loud as `LP24`, as Vital's do. **Tuning:** the loops are unchanged, so every mode of `Ladder` and `Dirty` rings at the same pitch as its low-pass, within 2 Hz from 200 Hz to 4 kHz; the ladder's `Notch` takes out the pitch it rings at, so it hardly self-oscillates, where `Dirty`'s output is its loop and still does. **Changing type:** the track keeps its mode, and a type that doesn't offer it plays its first, so going from `Ladder` at `HP24` through `LP` and back finds `HP24`, as the M14b design has a slot keep each of Distort's modes' settings. **Locks:** `MODE` is a stepped track setting, a place in the list of all twelve, from `00` `12 dB` to `0B` `-1 oct`, so steps can lock it and LFOs and envelopes can move it a whole step at a time. A lock for a mode the type doesn't offer plays its first, by the same rule, and on an MS-20 the lock is grey as a lock the track doesn't have. Unlike the filter type, a mode changes no settings, and it glides, so a lock is safe. **No clicks:** each channel moves its mix toward the mode's with the 10 ms smoother, every sample: the ladder's five weights, `Dirty`'s six, the clean filters' share of the second filter, and the diode's low cut. The second clean filter always runs on the clean types, so 24 dB fades in already settled. Jumping between two modes every 64 frames under a tone gives no bigger step between frames than either mode held, for every pair of neighbours and the first and last, clean and driven, where an unsmoothed mix gave 14 times as much. **Curve:** `response_db` takes the mode: the ladder's is the weighted sum of `(1 + jw)^(4 - i)` over `(1 + jw)^4 + k`; `Dirty`'s mixes its two-poles in the z-plane; the diode's now includes its input high-pass, worked out exactly from its updates; and the clean 24 dB multiplies in the second filter. Every mode of every type matches what comes out within 0.5 dB, the diode's within 1 dB as before. **Saving:** `filter_mode` is saved by name with the track, and `mode:06` in a step. **Unchanged:** each type's first mode is bit-identical to before, measured by summing a second of output on main and here. **Bounds:** at full `Res` with a full-level saw and the cutoff swept eight times a second, every mode stays under 2 for the character filters and 4 for the clean ones, and the aliasing test passes in each mode that passes its tone. **Cost:** a throwaway release-mode test on an Apple M5, one channel: a clean `LP` 5.6 ns a sample against 5.2 on main, for the second filter that always runs, `Ladder` 52 against 51, and `Diode`, `Dirty` and the MS-20's unchanged at 62, 52 and 31–34, in any mode. **Left out:** Braids' "punch" on the SVF, where the low-pass output raises the cutoff and the damping for an 808-style ring that drops in pitch, needs a continuous amount and a cutoff worked out every sample, so it's noted on #50 for later rather than made a mode. Added on 2026-11-08.
73. **Every list is one picker, with categories as a tree one level deep, after snacks.nvim's picker and its explorer** (#221). It's `Picker` in `input/picker.rs`, which holds the keys as well as the state, and `views/picker.rs`; `Picker::grouped`, its groups' column and the old `Focus::Groups` are gone, and machines, filter types and modes, FX commands, `Dest`, a slot's effects and settings, scales, views, tracks, guides, gates, chains, phrases, uses, the shape panel's rows, kits, samples and audio devices all go through it. **Items:** a name, a meaning drawn dim beside it, a category, and optionally a wider choice and a preview, so labels are no longer padded by hand, and the view lines the meanings up. **Categories:** each item names its category, and a category opens and closes, as a folder in snacks' explorer does. The one holding the current value opens with the cursor on that value, which is marked with a dim `•`, and the others start folded, as the explorer follows the current file; with no current value everything starts folded on the first row. A category of one item is listed as the item, with its category dim at the right, so `Dest`'s Machine, which holds only `Pitch`, takes no extra key, as it didn't before. **Keys:** in the list, `j` `k` with counts, `gg`, `G` and `5G`, `C-n` `C-p` and the arrows move; `l` and `Enter` open a category and go into its first item, or choose; `h` closes the category, from inside it, going up to its heading, or on it, as the explorer's `h` does; `/` goes to the search. Any other letter starts a search with itself, so `K` then `pl` still finds `Plaits`, but a name starting with a list key, such as `ladder`, `hld` or `kick`, now needs `/` first, which four tests now do. Digits then `Enter` search for the number and choose the best match, so a chain or phrase is still chosen by number; a count before `Enter` would otherwise mean nothing. In the search every letter types, `C-n` `C-p` and the arrows move, and `Enter` chooses. `Esc` clears a search and goes back to the tree, on the item it found with its category opened; with no search it closes, and a pending count or `g` is let go first, as in Vim. **Opening:** snacks opens most pickers on their input and its explorer on its list, and so does this: the samples, the tracks and the FX commands open on their search, as they're found by name and their tests type straight away, and every other list opens on the list. **Looks:** spartan and one colour: the title is the list's name with `matching/total` dim beside it, categories are dim headings with an arrow, `▸` folded or `▾` open, and their count, items are plain with their meaning dim, aligned whether nested or not, and a search's matched letters are in the picker's own colour, teal, bold. The search line is `> query_`, or a dim `>` with any count in the list. **Layout:** after snacks' `select`, a small centred box sized to the widest item and up to 16 rows; after its `default`, a list with a preview pane beside it, 40 wide, for a list whose items have previews, which only kits have for now. The box stays the same size as it's searched or opened, as it's sized from every item. **The kit picker:** `Space 8`, free beside `Space 5` for samples and `Space 7` for audio, and `:kit`, list the kit folders' kits under the folder each is in, such as `Ableton/Drum Machines` as the importer writes them (decision 68), which is the kit file's `category` without reading every kit to find it, and kits at a folder's top at the top. It opens on the kit in use, which `:kit`, `Enter` and `L` remember. Beside it, the kit under the cursor: its name, how many sounds and its `source`, and each sound's label, name and an 8 by 1 waveform, drawn as decision 58's, folded once for each file and kept for as long as the picker is open, and sent as images only while that kit is shown. `Enter` makes it the kit `Space 5` lists first, as `:kit name`, and `L`, the wider `l`, puts its sounds on tracks as `:kit name all`, as one undo step; `L` is only a key in a list with a wider choice, so `Limit` still types elsewhere, and a hint at the bottom says so. **Samples:** `Space 5` lists files by name under the folder they're in, and the kit's sounds under `Kit name` first, and opens on the track's own sample in its folder. **Left out:** auditioning a kit's sound by moving onto it in the preview, as #221's comment asks, needs the engine to play a file that's on no track, which it can't yet, so the preview has no focus of its own; the sample list has no preview, as loading every file the cursor passes on the edit thread would stall on a long one. Added on 2026-11-08. Decision 75 replaces the kit and sample lists here with the file explorer, which plays and previews samples.
74. **`Tab` completes the command line from the parser's own tables, with a list of matches above it, and `Up` and `Down` go back through the commands run, kept in `history.ron`, after Vim's `wildmenu` and history** (#225). **One source:** `input/command.rs` now keeps every command in `COMMANDS`, each with its name, what it does, what it takes and its own parse function, and every `:set` key in `SETTINGS` the same way; `parse` looks the name up there, so a command that isn't in the table can't run, and completion lists exactly the table. The words themselves come from the same places the parser reads: `RENDER_FLAGS`, `KIT_ALL`, the model's `LAUNCH_WORDS`, built from the names `Launch::named` reads, `ROOTS` and `ScaleKind::ALL`. A test completes every command from its first letter and every key from `:set `, and parses every line that any command's or key's completion makes. **What completes:** names, with what each does dim beside it; `:set` keys, with `=` added; their values, `on`, `off`, the scales, lower case with dashes as the guide writes them, and the roots; each command's words, `:launch`, `:track add|del`, `:scale fit`, and `off` for `:reset` and `:tswing`; `:render`'s flags once its path is typed, a flag given not offered again and nothing after `--rows`, which takes a number; kits by name from every kit folder in `settings.ron`, merged, one folder at a time as paths are, or by path when the argument starts with `/`, `~` or `.`, and `all` after a name that is a kit; and paths, one folder at a time with a `/` after a folder, `~` as the home folder, hidden files left out unless a `.` is typed. Paths only list what the command takes: folders for `:e`, `:w` and `:new`, as a project is a folder, folders and sample files for `:sample`, WAVs for `:render`, and kit files for `:kit`. Matching is by prefix, ignoring case, as Vim's file names are on macOS, rather than the picker's fuzzy search, as what completes has to start with what's typed; the match's own case is put in. Nothing adds a space after a name, as in Vim. **Matches:** one match is put in place. Several fill in the part they all share, as Vim's `wildmode=longest:full` does, and list above the command line, through the picker (decision 73) with the first highlighted, or the last for `Shift-Tab`, as Vim's does. `Tab` and `Shift-Tab`, the arrows and `C-n` `C-p` move through it, going round at the ends. Moving puts nothing in the line, unlike Vim's `wildmenu`, so typing narrows the list from what's typed, keeping the highlight, and closes it once nothing matches or a new word starts; `Enter` puts the highlighted match in and closes the list, leaving the line open, so a folder can be gone into with another `Tab`, and a second `Enter` runs it; `Esc` closes the list and keeps what's typed. The list only opens on `Tab`, as Vim's does and the issue asks, rather than as the author's blink.cmp setup shows it while typing. **Looks:** a small box sitting on the status line, just above the command line, with its names under the word they finish, or under the file name in a path, after Vim's popup menu, and a space inside its right edge: the list's name, such as `Commands`, `Settings`, `Paths`, `Kits` or the command or key, bold with the number of matches dim, then the picker's own rows, up to 10 before it scrolls, and no search line, as the command line is its search. **History:** each line run with `Enter` is kept, even one that doesn't parse, so it can be put right with `Up`; `Esc` keeps nothing. `Up` goes back from the newest, and with something typed only through lines starting with it, which is what was typed when `Up` was first pressed, as Vim's does; `Down` comes forward and back to what was typed. Typing or `Backspace` starts a new search from the line as it is. A line run again moves to the end rather than being kept twice, and the last 100 are kept. It's in `input/command_history.rs`, saved as a RON list in `history.ron` in the config folder, beside `settings.ron` and `audio.ron`, for this machine rather than for a song, written each time a line is added; a file that won't read starts an empty history and says why on the status line. Tests keep no file. **Left out:** the command line still has no cursor of its own, so `Left` and `Right` do nothing and completion is always at the end of the line, as it was typed before. Added on 2026-11-08.
75. **Files are chosen in one explorer, the picker in tree mode, after snacks.nvim's explorer, and moving onto a sample plays it on the current track through a preview voice** (#222). **The tree:** `Picker::explorer` in `input/picker.rs` keeps a node beside each item, with its path, its parent, its depth and, for a folder, whether it's open and what's in it once it's been read; a folder is read when it's first opened, so `~` costs one listing. `explorer.rs` says what each purpose lists: a project to open shows project folders, those with a `song.ron`, which are chosen rather than opened, and plain folders; a sample shows `SAMPLE_EXTENSIONS` files and folders; a kit shows `.ron` files, named without it, and folders; saving and rendering show folders only. Anything else is left out rather than dimmed, folders come first and each group is in order of name ignoring case, as snacks sorts them, and hidden files are left out until `H`. **Where it starts:** the top of the tree is a list of places, each with why it's there dim beside it: `recent` for the last three folders something was chosen in for that purpose, kept in `places.ron` beside `history.ron` (projects are opened and saved in the same ones), then the project's folder for samples, kits and renders, or the folder it's in for opening and saving, the settings' sample or kit folders, `here` for the folder the program was run from, and `home`. One that isn't there is passed over, and one that's the same place as an earlier one, however it's written, is listed once. **Following:** it opens on the current file, the track's sample, the kit in use or the project, opening the folders down to it from the place that holds it most closely, and marks it with `•`; a folder followed is opened too. With nothing to follow, the first place is opened with the cursor on the first thing in it, so there's something to see. `Space 5` puts the kit's sounds first, under `Kit 909`, a group given up front, open, and opens on the track's sound in it or the kit's first. A long path at the top keeps its end, after an ellipsis. **Keys,** as snacks' and as every other list's: `j` `k` with counts, `gg` `G`, `l` (or Right) opens a folder and goes into it or chooses a file, `h` (or Left) closes the folder, from inside it or on it, `Backspace` goes up, making the place the cursor is under its own parent, with it open inside and the cursor where it was, `H` shows hidden files, reading what's open again and keeping the cursor, `P` shows or hides the preview, `A` turns playing off or on, `/` searches, and `Esc` clears a search and then closes. `Enter` is `l`, except where a folder is being chosen, where it chooses the one under the cursor and `l` opens it. `I` for ignored files is left out, as there's no ignore file to read. **Search:** exact, ignoring case, as the author's explorer is set up, rather than the picker's fuzzy search, across what's been read, open or not, with each match under the folders it's in and the cursor on the first match rather than a folder; it doesn't read folders that haven't been opened, as searching `~` would read the whole disk. **Line numbers:** relative, dim, down the left, with the cursor's own number on its row, as Vim's `number` with `relativenumber`, so `12j` can be read off. **Looks:** the same box, a fixed 52 wide and 18 rows whether the preview shows or not, so it doesn't change size as folders open; folders have `▸` or `▾`, and the title has no count, as it would only count what's been read. **Preview,** on by default except where a folder is being chosen: a sample's name, its length, its own rate and whether it's mono or stereo (`load_sample_with_info` gives `SampleInfo`), and its waveform 28 by 4, decision 58's drawing sent as a kitty image, 28 being as many columns as the program's table of kitty's placeholder marks held then, which was only the start of the protocol's 297 (decision 93); a project's name, its tempo and tracks, and when its `song.ron` was saved, roughly, as `Saved 3 hours ago`; a kit's sounds, as before. **Reading samples:** `loading.rs` reads them on a thread of its own, started when the first is asked for, which reads only the newest asked for, so moving quickly past a folder doesn't queue each one, and keeps the last four read, as a long file can be large; the preview says `Loading` until it's in, and the main loop takes it in each frame. `App::finish_loading` waits for it, for tests. **Playing as it moves,** as the M8's and Elektron's sample browsers do: moving onto a sample plays it, once read, on the current track, through its filter, levels and sends, from its start to its end or until the cursor moves, `A` or `Esc`; it isn't put on the track until it's chosen, and `A`'s setting lasts for the session. The engine plays a file on no track through the preview voices: `Handle::play_file` sends an `Arc` of the file read on the edit thread through a queue of its own and a `PlayFile` command after it, so it starts in order with `StopFile`, and the callback only plays it. It plays under one of two ids no pool sample has, in two places, so the last file fades out on its own sound while the next starts, and the one it replaces goes back through another queue to be freed on the edit thread; it's taken only when there's room to send that back, so nothing is freed or allocated on the audio thread. A track that makes no sound gives the file back. **Saving:** `:w` before the project has a folder, and `:new`, open the explorer on folders beside the project; `Enter` chooses one, and the command line opens with `w ~/folder/` filled in, for the name to be typed with Tab's path completion (decision 74), and `Enter` saves. `App::suggested_name` is where #224's random names go; it's empty until then. `:w` with a folder still saves in place, as Vim's does, rather than asking where. `:render` with no path does the same in the project's folder, with the project's name and `.wav` filled in, or the name alone for `--stems`, and any flags given kept after it. **Where it's used:** `Space 5` and `:sample` with no path, `:sample folder`, opened on that folder alone, `Space 8` and `:kit`, `:e`, `:w`, `:new` and `:render` with nothing after them. Typed paths work as before. `picking.rs`'s `find_samples`, which listed up to 2000 files four folders deep, and `kits_in` are gone, and the old flat sample list with them. Added on 2026-11-08.
76. **Projects and renders are given random names, an adjective and a noun joined by a hyphen, as tracker-ce's name generator makes them, and `C-r` rolls a new one on any name line** (#224). **The words:** our own lists, not tracker-ce's, whose licence restricts clones: 275 adjectives and 266 nouns in `crates/vt-model/src/names/`, one to a line, read with `include_str!`, leaning on textures, weather, places, materials, motion and music, all plain lower case letters and safe for anyone; a test checks each list has only letters and no word twice. **Names:** `vt_model::names::Names` makes `dusty-breakdown`, or one time in ten two nouns, as tracker-ce does, such as `tape-harbor`, never the same word twice. **Seeding:** from the clock, by SplitMix64, apart from the song's seed, so naming something doesn't change what `CHA` or the shape panel's random values do. **Never reused:** `taken_in` reads the target folder's names without their extensions and in lower case, as macOS folders ignore case, and a name in it is rolled again, up to 1000 times, then given the lowest free number. **Where they go:** the app makes a name when it starts, `App::project_name` until the project has a folder, and `App::suggested_name`, decision 75's hook, now takes the folder chosen: `:w` with no folder fills in that name after the folder, so `Enter` saves it, or another if the folder has it already; `:new` fills in a fresh one; `:render` fills in the project's name, its folder's or the random one before it's saved, rather than `loop`, and **numbers it** if that file is there, `dusty-breakdown-2.wav`, rather than rolling a random name, so renders stay named after their project and an earlier one isn't written over, as Ableton's export names its file after the set. The window title still says only `vimtracker` before there's a folder, so it's clear nothing is saved. **The key:** `C-r` on the command line, after tracker-ce's random key on its name keyboard. It puts a new name in place of the last part of the path on a `:w`, `:new` or `:render` line, keeping the folder, an extension, and `:render`'s flags, adds `.wav` to a render with no name yet but not for `--stems`, and does nothing on any other line. In Vim, `C-r` on the command line inserts a register, but vimtracker's registers hold steps, which can't go on a command line, so nothing it would mean is lost, and the normal-mode `C-r`, redo, is a separate table; decision 74's `Tab`, `Up`, `Down` and the list's `C-n` `C-p` don't use it. **Left out:** kit and preset saving (#157) and recording have no name lines yet; they'll start from `Names` when they do, as will the project page's Name. Added on 2026-11-08. Decision 77 changes this: the name is the song's, the line saving somewhere new starts with it, numbered if the folder has it, rather than another random name, and the window is titled `vimtracker: name` from the start.
77. **The project page, on `Space 9`, shows and changes the song's own settings in rows laid out as the track page's, and the project's name is the song's, which a new folder takes on save-as** (#223), after the M8's Project view, Elektron's project settings and the Polyend Tracker's project screen in tracker-ce's `projectEditor`. It's `View::Project` and `Mode::Project`, `project_page.rs` and `views/project.rs`, with its own rows in the binding table, so `Space k` and the popup list them. **Where:** `Space 9`, the next free digit, as `Space 8` is now the kit picker; the audio page keeps `Space 7`, and the project page has one row for it rather than a copy. **Rows,** in two columns, how it plays on the left and how notes go in on the right: `Name` and `Folder` across the top, then `BPM` and `Scale`, `Swing` and `Root`, `Launch` and `Grid`, `Reset` and `Auto-jump`, then `Seed` and `Limiter` on the left alone, `Audio`, and the actions `Save`, `Save as`, `Render`, `Open` and `New`, each with its command dim beside it. **One path:** each row changes what its `:` command does, through it: `=` `-` `+` `_` make the `Line` the command would and run it, `K`'s lists hold `Pick::Line`, and what `i` types goes through `parse` as `bpm 137.63`, `launch 12`, `reset 0x40` or `set skip=3`, so it takes what the command takes and a mistake says what the command says. So undo is where the command has it: the tempo, swing, launch, reset, seed, limiter, grid and name are undo steps, and the scale and auto-jump aren't, as decisions 24 and 48 keep them out of undo. **Steps:** the tempo moves 1 BPM, and 10 for a large step; `l` goes onto its fine part, the digits after the point, drawn dim as the track page's fine digits are, which moves a hundredth and a tenth, carrying into the whole, and typed is the digits after the point, so `63` is `.63`; it shows two digits, or three where `:bpm` set a thousandth. Swing moves as a track's does, 1% and 5%; reset a step and a bar, down to off; the seed 1 and 10. Launch, the scale, its root and the grid are lists, one choice at a time either way as the filter type is, a launch with no name going to the named one beside it. The limiter and auto-jump are on and off: `=` on, `-` off, and `K` or `i` switch them, as the mixer's mute does. `x` puts each back as a new project has it: 120 BPM, or the fine part to `.00`, swing 50, `1bar`, no reset, seed 0, limiter on, scale off, root C, a grid of a beat and auto-jump off. **The scale on the page:** the Scale row's first choice is `off`, so it turns the scale on and off as well as choosing its kind, and the root changes only the root, unlike `:set root`, which turns the scale on, so moving through roots with it off doesn't. **Reset** is a section's, so the row is the cursor's section's, and says which once there's more than one. **Moving:** as the track page's (decision on row-preserving moves, #185): `j` and `k` with counts keep the column they left, so going down the right column past `Seed` and back comes back to it, `gg` and `G` with a count go to a row, `h` `l` `0` `$` go along it, `l` stops on the tempo's fine part, and `h` from `Scale` lands on it. `Enter`, beside `K`, does an action or opens the audio page, and `i` on anything that isn't typed does as `K` does. **Actions:** `Save` is `:w`, which with no folder yet opens the explorer and name line (decision 75); `Save as` opens them whatever, saving a copy to carry on in; `Render`, `Open` and `New` are `:render`, `:e` and `:new` with nothing after them, so `Open` and `New` refuse with unsaved changes as those do. **The name:** `Song.name`, saved in `song.ron`, `#[serde(default)]` so a song saved before it has none and takes its folder's when it's opened, which is no conversion, as nothing old is rewritten until it's saved. It's the song's own rather than the folder's, as the M8 keeps a project's name apart from its file and Elektron's project names are their own. A new project is given decision 76's random name in the song, so `App::unsaved_name` is gone and `App::project_name` is the song's name. Saving to a new folder, `:w path` or the explorer's name line, and `:new path`, name the project after the folder, which those lines start with, so the two only differ if another name is typed there, as Ableton names a set after its file on Save As. Renaming on the page doesn't move the folder, as moving folders on disk under a running project would be risky: `:w` still saves where it is, and `Save as` starts with the new name. The line saving somewhere new starts with the project's name, and **numbers it** if the folder has it, `dusty-breakdown-2`, as a render is numbered, rather than rolling another random name as decision 76 did, since the name may now be one chosen on purpose; `:new` still starts with a fresh random name. A name is up to 32 characters, with no `/` or control characters and not starting with a dot or a space or ending with a space, as it has to make a folder; `i` on Name starts with it as it is, `C-u` clears it, and `C-r` rolls a new one, not one in the folder beside this one. **The window title** is `vimtracker: name` from the start, which replaces decision 76's plain `vimtracker` before there's a folder; the Folder row says `Not saved yet`, or the folder, a long one keeping its end after an ellipsis, with `saved` or `unsaved changes`. The explorer's project preview shows the song's name where it has one. **Looks:** spartan, as the audio page: a bold `Project`, names dim, values plain, the cursor's in the cursor's colour, a dim root while the scale is off, blank lines between the name, the settings, the audio and the actions, and nothing drawn; the right column starts 40 in, within the usual 120. Added on 2026-11-08. Decision 155 moves the limiter off this page, into the master's Output device.
78. **A track's `Gate` is how much of a note's last pulse it lasts, from 1% to 100%, and `Acid`'s starts at 50%** (part 1 of #227, the time model's gate length). **The setting:** `Gate` is one of the track's own settings in the registry, `gate` in files and `LEN` on a step, listed under Machine with Pitch, so an LFO or envelope can move it and a step locks it for its row's notes, as Elektron's `LEN` sets a trig's length. On `00` to `FF`, `FF` is 100% and `80` is 50%, and `00` still gives 1%, so a note always sounds. It's in the Sound page's right-hand column under `Choke`, shown as a percentage: `=` and `-` move it a percent, `+` and `_` ten, and `i` takes a percent. Dimmed, it's the machine's own, which `x` goes back to. **What it measures:** from the note's start, as Elektron and the Metropolix measure a length, to `Gate` of the way through the note's last pulse. A `SGL`'s last pulse is its row's last, so `SGL3` at 50% lasts two and a half steps. A `MLT`'s notes last a pulse each, so each lasts `Gate` of its pulse, the Metropolix's staccato multiple. `HLD` ignores it and ties into the next row's note, which closes its own gate, and `RST` is unchanged. A delayed note's length counts from its delayed start. #227 left open whether a `SGL` takes `Gate` of its first pulse or of its whole row. The first pulse was rejected because 100% has to hold a note as it did before: a `SGL4` would jump from under one pulse at 99% to four at 100%, and on `Acid` a long note that doesn't slide would need an `OFF`. Scaling the whole row was rejected because it halves a long note, where a gap before the next note is what a shorter gate means. The last pulse is how a 303 plays a tied note, holding its gate through the tied steps and closing it halfway through the last, so `SGL2` on `Acid` is a note and a tie. **How a note ends:** the gate closing lets the note go as `OFF` does, starting the release of its envelopes, so the release shapes the gap; it isn't a cut. It also ends a `RET`'s repeats, which play only while the gate's open, as a ratchet on a short note stays short; an `OFF` or `KIL` before it still ends the note sooner. At 100% nothing is scheduled, so a song with no `Gate` set plays as before, sample for sample, and the golden renders are unchanged. The length is worked out on the step's time without swing, so a swung step's gate isn't stretched or squeezed with it; the Groove (#227's part 2) can revisit this. **`Acid`:** its `Gate` starts at `80`, as a 303's gate closes about halfway through each step unless it slides (Whittle, Open303's `setNoteLength`), and its own release, from decision 46, takes it 40 dB down 11 ms after the gate closes and 60 dB down in 18 ms, measured in `tests/gate_length.rs`. A slide is `HLD` on the row that slides away, as a 303's slide is set on the step it leaves (#220's second item), so it holds the gate into the note it slides to, which then closes its own halfway. So that its filter and accent sweep still carry from note to note across the gaps, as on a 303, a released `Acid` voice keeps running silently until its sweep has drained, rather than stopping when its envelope's release ends; with the gaps, a run of accents builds up less than when every note holds, as the sweep charges only while the gate's open. Every other machine starts at 100%, so pads and held notes are unchanged. This is the general setting that replaces #220's `Acid`-only one. Speed and the Groove, the rest of #227, come later. Added on 2026-11-08. Decision 79 gives a row a length of its own in its gate cell, which `LEN` no longer sets, and makes what moves `Gate` move each note's length up or down from its own.
79. **A row's gate cell holds how long its note lasts, and what moves `Gate` moves that length up or down rather than replacing it** (part of #227, after decision 78). The author decided that a note's length belongs to the note's part of the row, and that FX columns and lanes modulate it. **The cell:** a third part after the gate and its pulses, as in `SGL1 80`, on the `00` to `FF` scale `Gate` uses, drawn dim, and `--` with none, which plays at the track's `Gate`. The whole cell is 7 characters, so the first FX column starts 3 further right; at 120 columns the phrase view still has its overview of the other tracks beside it. In step text the length is a field after the gate's, `C-4 64 SGL1 80 cut:40`, as two hex digits can't be taken for an FX slot, and a row without one is written as before. It's a cursor stop of its own in the gate's column group, as an FX value's fine digits are in their slot's: `l` goes onto it, two hex digits in Insert or after `r` set it, `=` and `-` move it by one and `+` and `_` by 16, `x` and `Backspace` take it away, and `K` there chooses the gate and keeps it. Being in the gate's group, it's copied, pasted and merged with the cell, and `x` on the gate's own part empties the cell, length and all. A length typed into an empty cell makes it one pulse of `SGL`, which is how it played. `r` across a selection fills it on the rows with a note, as velocity is filled, and `.` puts it on another row's cell, keeping that row's gate. An empty length moved with `=` or `-` starts from the track's `Gate`, as that's what it plays at, and only on a row with a note, so a selection doesn't put cells on empty rows. The shape panel doesn't fill it yet. **The rule:** a row's own length is the base. Without one, the base is the track's `Gate` as it is when the note starts, a `LEN` lock or a lane's value included, so `LEN` only moves rows without a length. Modulation adds to the base, as an LFO's offset adds to every other setting's held value (`offsets` in `track_state.rs`): an LFO or envelope on `Gate`, and `LDLT`, both on rows with a length of their own and without. The sum is kept from 1% to 100%, and at 100% nothing is scheduled, as before. `HLD` ignores all of it; a lock on a `HLD` row carries into the note it ties into, which closes its own gate with it, as a tie is still the same note. **`LDLT`** is a step setting of the track's, `gatedelta` in files, named "Gate up or down" and listed under Machine beside `LEN`, with "Gate length, up or down from the note's own" as its meaning. It's centred on `80`, which leaves a note alone, and one step of it is one step of `Gate`, so `00` takes half a pulse off and `FF` adds half, shown from `-50 %` to `+50 %`. It sweeps, so it has fine digits and glides. It's a registry setting of the track's whose held value is used as an offset, rather than a lockable modulation value like `DEP1`. `DEP1` belongs to an LFO, sets how far it swings and has its base on the Mod page; a change to a note's length has no modulator to belong to. As one of the track's settings it gets locks, holds, glides, lanes, `K` and the step text from the registry with nothing new, and as its base is always `80` it needs nothing on the track or the track page. It's left out of the LFOs' and envelopes' `Dest`, as moving it would only move `Gate`, which they can already. The label was free, as was `GOFS`, the other considered; `LDLT` sits with `LEN`. **Live recording** (decision 55) writes a held note's length in its gate cell rather than in an `OFF`, and leaves the row's pulses as they are, as recording mustn't reshape a loop or change its length. The length is what was held of the row's last pulse, at most `FE`, so the note ends where it was let go. A note held past the row's end is `FF`, which rings on until the next note or something else ends it, as a held note does. A note let go before its row's last pulse, as a tap on a row of several pulses is, gets `00`, the least the cell can say, so it lasts just into that pulse. While the key is down the cell is `FF`, and a note still held when its row comes round again ends there. The search for an FX column to put an `OFF` in is gone. **Alternatives:** a length only as `LEN`, decision 78's, is what this changes, as a lane of `LEN` replaced every note's own length rather than moving it. A first version made a recorded row last as many pulses as its hold, rounded to the nearest step, so a hold was exact to the cell's resolution. The author rejected it on review, as it moved every later row and changed the track's loop length, so the track drifted against the others. A note held past its row now rings on to the next one rather than stopping where it was let go, which is the cost. Added on 2026-11-08.
80. **A Groove is a track's feel: a pattern of 1 to 16 pushes in ticks, how late or early each step lands, with a velocity for each step and a seeded humanise, chosen for the project, for a track, or on a step with `GRV`, and swing is now grooves** (part 2 of #227, the time model's feel), after the M8's Groove view and its "Common Groove Examples" (manual pp. 26 and 81), Ableton's groove pool and the MPC 60's swing. **The pattern:** `Groove`, in `vt-model`'s `groove.rs`, has 1 to 16 steps, counted from the start of the running count. Each has a push in ticks, 240 to a step, from -120 to 120, half a step, as far as swing at 75% went, and a velocity in percent, 100 unless changed, up to 200. Its humanise moves each step at random up to 24 ticks either way, a tenth of a step, from a generator of the track's own seeded from the project's seed as `CHA`'s is, but on another stream, so a render repeats and `CHA` rolls as it did. **Pushes sum to zero, as the first step stays on the grid.** #227 agreed that pushes sum to zero, so a groove never changes the tempo or how long a loop takes, which is the M8's rule that a groove's tick counts add up to the pattern's. Written as the M8 writes them, as how much longer each step is, today's swing at 66% is `+77 -77`, which sums to zero but reads as the first step being late when it's the second. Written as where each step lands, which is #227's push, it's `0 +77`, which doesn't sum to zero, and pushes centred to sum to zero, `-38 +39`, would move the downbeat and change every swung render. The two ways of writing it are the same thing: lengths that add up to the pattern's are landings whose first is 0. So a push is where its step lands, as Ableton's grooves, the MPC's swing and Elektron's micro-timing show it, and the first step's push is always 0, as it's the one the others are pushed from. The rule then can't be broken, so nothing is rebalanced and nothing refused on saving, and changing a push moves that one step, where on the M8 changing one moves the next with it. A push can be either way, so a step can land early. **Presets:** Straight; Swing 52, 54, 56, 58, 60, 62, 64, 66 and 70, each two steps with the second as late as the old `swing_ticks` put it, so they play as `:swing` did at those percentages, frame for frame, and the golden render with swing at 66.7%, now a groove of the project's own, is unchanged; MPC 54, 58, 62, 66 and 71, a bar of 16 steps of the MPC 60's sixteenth swing at its own settings, each landing on the MPC's clock of 96 to a quarter, 10 of our ticks, so MPC 66 is the triplet, 80 ticks, where Swing 66 is 77; Late last, the M8's "Swing Last Step", a bar on the grid but its last step, 40 ticks late, as the M8's `07 05` at its 24 to a quarter; and two bars of push and pull, Push, with the off-beat eighths 12 ticks early and the sixteenths between them 6, and Pull, with the backbeats 20 ticks late and the sixteenths between 8. Every preset's velocities are 100%, as one that quietened some steps would quieten a kick on them too. A groove is kept in songs by its name, as settings are by key, so adding a preset doesn't move the others. **Where it's chosen:** `Song.groove`, the project's, Straight in a new project, and `Track.groove`, a track's own or none to follow the project's, in place of `swing` on both. `:groove name` and `:tgroove name` set them, `:tgroove off` follows the project's again, and either alone opens a list; a name is written as `swing-66`, `mpc-58` or `custom-1`, with case, spaces and hyphens ignored, and Tab completes them. `:swing 66` is kept as a shorthand for the Swing groove at that percent, or Straight at 50, and `:swing 66.7` is refused, as there's no groove at it; `:tswing` is gone. On the track page `Swing` is now `Groove`: `=` and `-` move through the list one at a time from what it plays, `K` and `i` list them with the project's first, it's dimmed while it follows the project's, and `x` follows it again. The status line says `Groove Swing 66`, with `(track)` after a track's own. No song is converted, so one saved with `swing` plays straight. **`GRV` on a step:** `groove`, labelled `GRV`, is a registry setting of the track's, a list whose first choice is "Project", the project's groove, followed by every groove in order, and whose held value starts as the track's own, or `00` with none. So locks, holds, lanes, `K`, the picker and the step text come from the registry with nothing new. It's listed under Machine with `LEN`, and left out of the LFOs' and envelopes' `Dest`, as a groove is chosen rather than swept. Unlike any other setting it's read before its step plays, as it says when the step lands: a `GRV` on the step's row, or in a lane at the step, gives the step its groove; if not, and the row's first pulse has a note, which will end any lock, the held value does; otherwise whatever the track holds. A lock lasts its note, as Elektron's do, so a fill goes straight with `GRV 01` on each of its rows, or with `GRV 01h` at its start and a hold of the track's groove after, as the M8's `GRV` holds until the next. The engine only looks for it on rows when the song has a `GRV` somewhere, so other songs don't pay for the walk to the row twice. **What it counts against:** the running count every track shares, the one swing counted, from the song's start or the last reset, a section that resets or `:reset`, not each track's own loop or each phrase's rows. So tracks of different lengths swing together, and the groove stays on the beat; a 7-row phrase drifts against a 2-step swing, as a phrase of that length drifts against a real swung beat. A section that doesn't reset carries the count on, as swing did, so a groove counts from the start of the section the count began in rather than each section's own. Counting by a phrase's rows, the M8's way, could be a per-track option later. **How it meets the rest:** each pulse of a row is a step, so a `MLT`'s pulses each get their own push and velocity. Lanes are read when their step lands, so they're grooved, as swing grooved them. `DEL`, `KIL`, `OFF` and `RET` count from where the step lands. A step's velocity scales the note's own, rounded and kept at `7F` at most, so it can make an accent or lose one. A note's gate length, decision 78's, which used the step's time without swing, now follows the grooved step: it runs from the note's start to where its last pulse lands, then its `Gate` share of how long the groove makes that pulse, from where it lands to where the next does, so the long first step of a swung pair holds its note longer and the short second shorter. With Straight that's the old sum, so unswung renders are unchanged. Humanise moves a note's start and end together. Live recording, decision 55, puts a note on the row whose grooved start is nearer, writes it once that row has landed so it isn't played twice, and writes its length as a share of its last pulse as the groove makes it, with the track's own groove or the project's; a step's `GRV` isn't looked at. #146's nudge will add on top: grid, then groove, then nudge. **Early steps:** a step pushed early has to be played before its place on the grid, so the engine works every step out ahead of its place by as far as any groove the song can play lands one early: the project's, the tracks' own and any a `GRV` in the song chooses, found in `Published::new` as `lead`. With none early it's 0, and steps are worked out on the grid as they always were, so nothing else moves. With a lead, a section change, a cue and a launch that stops a track happen that much early, up to half a step; the playhead stays on the step heard until the next one's place on the grid; a render works its pass's first step out again from the top; and a step can land no later than half a step late, which is no later than the next is worked out, so steps always play in order. #146's early nudge can use the same. **On screen:** nothing changes in the grid. Steps are drawn on their rows, and the playhead moves on the grid. **The project page** keeps the project's own grooves, eight of them, `Custom 1` to `Custom 8`, in `Song.grooves`, each a bar on the grid until it's made. Its `Swing` row is now `Groove`, a list as `Launch` is.
